ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e206	Honourable co-representatives! Former Stable Representative from Hedmark and the Workers' Party, Else Marie Myhren Repål, for us in Stortinget also known as Else Bakke, died on 19 April, 85 years old. She represented Hedmark for over 12 years, from 1969. She was a member of the Administrative Committee, the Preparatory Powers Committee, the Social Committee and the Powers Committee. She also met as an alternate between 1981 and 1985. She was an alternate member of the Interparliamentary Union, IPU, and was a delegate to the 1977 UN General Assembly. When Else Marie Myhren Repål ended after about twelve years on Stortinget, she saw with joy back at the time of the fall. The joy was not quite as great that day in 1969 when she was told that she had a safe place on the election list. There was something quite different than what she had asked when she was asked to stand there. Her condition to say yes was just that she should be standing far down on the list, and that she was sure not to be elected. It was also written by Hamar Arbeiderblad. And she admitted in newspaper interviews that she was amazed when she first came into the Stortinget. But later she never regretted that she had said yes. Else Marie Myhren Repål soon found himself well at ease at Stortinget. In particular, she got involved in matters where she got help to poor individuals. In retrospect, she looked back at the bulk work with great joy. Else Marie Myhren Repål was well-like and had many friends here at Stortinget. She was, among other things, Chairman of the Stockholm Welfare Committee, such as: stood for the operation of pottery, trim and the bathroom. Being a big-time politician is an unsafe profession. It also got Else Marie Myhren Repetition. In 1981 she was pushed down on the electoral list. She ended up in sixth place and thus became the first alternate. Else Marie Myhren Repål assured that she was not bitter, and said once: I had been sitting for three periods and that might be enough. I had to accept that other and younger people came in. Otherwise Else Marie Myhren Repetitious political to live as a 15-year-old. Among other things, she met Reulf Steen and became one of the most enthusiastic in the local AUF team. Then she started a women's group on Rena, and it opened up space both in the social board and in the child's protection board. As one of the first female representatives from Hedmark, Else Marie Myhren Repet became a pioneer at Stortinget. She became a firehouse for her heart matters, which concerned welfare issues for most people. We thank Else Marie Myhren Repål for a long working day, and brighten the peace of her memory. The representatives heard the President's commemorative speech.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e320	At the request of the Committee on Energy and the Environment, the President will propose that the debate be limited to 1 hour and 30 minutes and that speaking time be allocated to the groups as follows: Working Party 30 minutes, Right 25 minutes, Advance Party 15 minutes, Christian People's Party 5 minutes, Center Party 5 minutes, Left 5 minutes, Socialist Left Party 5 minutes and Environment Party Green 5 minutes. Furthermore, the President will propose that six lines of reply be given after the intervention of a member of the Government within the allocated speaking time, and that those on the list of speakers, in addition to the allocated speaking time, have a speaking time of up to 3 minutes. – It shall be deemed to have been adopted.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e329	I would first like to thank the Committee on good and constructive cooperation. In my opinion, the attitude gives a good picture of where the parties agree, and where there is real disagreement in the policy of the country's main industry. Global population growth and wealth development are increasing in demand for energy. Oil and gas will be an important part of the energy mix in the foreseeable future. Growth in energy consumption, together with the need to replace declining production, suggests that in the foreseeable future there will be a market for the oil and gas resources in Norway. We will therefore be an oil nation for many decades. Ever since the very beginning, it has been an overall objective that the Norwegian petroleum resources should benefit the entire population and that it should lead to the creation of value throughout the country. The policy pursued to achieve these objectives has produced results. 240 000 jobs throughout the country are attributable to the activity of this industry. 27 pst. of the income of the country in 2014 and 29 p.m. of the total investments in the country came in the petroleum sector. This industry has become the country's most important, and the state of the industry affects the whole country. Large investments, long lead-time in development projects and long-term performance horizons make this industry more sensitive to political risks than others. Stability and predictability are therefore important when companies are required to make their investment decisions. I am therefore pleased to conclude that there is still a broad consensus on the overall lines of our petroleum policy. There is unanimous support for the above-mentioned overall objectives, a broad majority of which indicates that, for many decades to come, petroleum activities will play a central role in Norwegian value creation, employment, technology development and societal development. The policy drawn up in the Stoltenberg government's big message A industry for the future, continued by the sitting government. This is an expression of political stability and predictability across changing governments. There is an overall committee, with the exception of the Greens/Greens/EFA, which points to the need to continue the work on emissions reductions from this industry and the development of low-emission technologies to enable Norwegian petroleum activities to deliver oil and gas in the 2050s. The development of the Sverdrup field is becoming in many ways an area for the operationalisation of our long-term objectives. This field requires huge investments, it has a very long production time, and it is a fact that many of those who are going to join in shutting down the field are not yet born. There are in particular two aspects which have taken on board the majority of the Committee. The importance of the project for the Norwegian supplier industry and the climate perspective lies in the fact that the Sverdrup field is to be driven by power from land, but also that this energy solution provides the basis for three other fields in the area of the Utsira Height to be driven by power from land. Contracts have been awarded so far for 27 out of a total of 117 billion. kr in connection with the construction phase of the project 1 – Aker Solutions, Aibel, Kværner, ABB and Odfjell Drilling have received a big job. In today's economic situation, these missions make an important contribution to our ability to maintain capacity and competence in the supplier industry. In the Committee's comments on force from the country, we have continued the unanimity of the Innst. 237 S for 2013–2014. The Committee acknowledges that it is most appropriate for the operator and the licensees to see the location and dimensioning of area resolution for power from land in the context of the development of construction stage 2, but underlines that the area resolution will be in place in 2022. To this end, the Committee supports proposals to amend the conditions, where we take out what can be seen as a binding on progress in building stage 2, and states that the solution should be in place by 2022. The heat demand in the Edvard Grieg field has become a topic in this matter. The Committee expects a qualification process for electric boilers to be carried out prior to the decision on how to meet the heat demand. To sum up, there is a broad consensus on the long lines of the petroleum policy, and there is a broad connection to the plan for the development and operation of Johan Sverdrup. Let me say something about those issues where there is political disagreement. This is primarily about how we are going to meet the dramatic situation we have today in the country's most important industry. Only in this week has it been announced that 4000 jobs are disappearing in oil companies and the supplier industry. DNB Markets has calculated that more than 20 000 jobs have been lost since January last year. In my conversations with employees in oil companies, representatives of the supplier industry, Norwegian Industry and the trade union movement, the situation is described as serious and dramatic. Maintenance is postponed, projects are put on hold, suppliers are pushed to, and past, their outermost limits at price, and recruitment to the industry is stopped. It is obvious to everyone who wants to see that the management of low oil prices by oil companies is not sustainable. There is a broad consensus that the activity in the industry will resume in a few years. The working party fears that such a rise will trigger an upward cost spiral and that capacity and competence will be rebuilt in a short time, which will be reduced in these days. This is not the first time that the oil nation of Norway has experienced the oil industry being in bad weather. In previous downsurges, labor party governments have acted, as we have, for example. In 1993, when we set up NORSOK, in 1997, when we took the initiative to set up INTSOK, and in 2001, when the KonKraft cooperation was established. All these measures have worked, and none of them would exist without political initiative and – let me add – political will. This has been a timely effort to achieve our objective for the oil industry, as described in the Petroleum Act. The Resource Manager shall provide the country with income and contribute to ensuring well-being, employment and a better environment and strengthen Norwegian business and industrial development, taking into account the interests of district policy and other activities. We therefore ask the government to assess and come back to the budget for 2016, with eight measures that we believe will help to mitigate the fall in activity and contribute to ensuring competence and capacity. We want stronger political participation in KonKraft cooperation and believe that the department should resume participation in this cooperation. All experience suggests that this type of cooperation between industry, trade unions and authorities contributes positively to challenges such as those we now experience. We believe that authorities need to make greater use of the tools they have to ensure that time-critical and profitable activities are carried out in times of free capacity in the supplier industry. This applies to well ploughing, maintenance and modifications, and to enhanced extraction measures. We believe that there is a need to assess the functioning of the permit code for this industry, and we believe that there is a need to look at what can be done to ensure that the relevant lines of secondary schools are not reduced as a result of the fact that companies are not recruiting in the current situation. We know that in a few years' time we'll see a wave of pensioners out of this industry, which can help to reinforce the negative effects of the brutal deflation we see today. We have also seen for a long time a trend with delays and large exceedances in development projects. The report by the Directorate-General for Oil on this subject shows that delays and exceedances, for example: come as a result of a lack of knowledge of NORSOK standards, HMS legislation and shortcomings in the prequalification and follow-up of suppliers. We have repeatedly seen Norwegian suppliers lose their bid against Asian shipyards, where the final result is costs far beyond the original tender and delivery much later than intended. This is of course a problem for Norwegian shipyards. It is felt that competition is not real. It is also a problem for Norwegian society, as 89 pst. the costs are depreciated on the tax. In other words, society has 89 pst. of the risk. The Working Party therefore considers that it is appropriate to investigate how, through closer follow-up to development projects, governments can ensure that contract strategies and implementation contribute to the achievement of our overall objectives of petroleum policy. We have also noted the concerns of both the industry and the trade union movement as to whether the HMS standard is given sufficient weight when contracts are awarded. Korea, which has received many mandates for Norwegian capital, has a death rate per 100,000 yearly works of 12.5, while the corresponding rate in Norwegian industry is 0.9. No one should believe that our high level of health, environment and safety comes of itself, or that it is free. When three deaths are recorded on the Goliath platform on the yard, it should call our attention. We can't accept that life and health become a competitive factor, where the person who downgrades this wins the bidding. Let me also add: we have seen that the unions at the Hyundai yard in Korea have now actually asked the Norwegian oil fund to withdraw from the investments in Hyundai, with a background in the poor HMS situation. We are therefore asking the government to assess whether all or part of our HMS legislation can also be enforced in the construction of installations for Norwegian sockets, in order to ensure that our ambitions to be world leaders on HMS also follow the projects from the start until they are ready to install and operate on a socket. It's not the first time we've had a setback that we've seen. What is different now is that we have a Right Party government at the rudder that minimizes the situation and leaves to the market to solve the problems. The Council of State hassles with those who believe that action needs to be taken, reminds me of Marie Antoinette's response to the French people's demand for bread: Can they not eat cakes, then? With this I address the suggestions that the Workers' Party and others are behind in the setting.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e369	The representative Per Rune Henriksen has taken up the suggestions he referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e372	At 2: 00 p.m., September night in 1969, the telephone called the Norwegian head of Phillips, Ed Crump, in Stavanger. It was the rigging director of "Ocean Viking", Ed Seabourn, who called. Listen, he said, I think I have oil all over the North Sea, but the well is under control. At the same time, Seabourn had sent a message to the office in Stavanger: ‘I hereby declare that the North Sea, from here to the North Pole, is a large oil basin’. With these words, Norway's history as an oil nation was sealed. The findings of «Ocean Viking» and Phillips this night in September came at the tame of what had proved to be a very disappointing second concession round on Norwegian pedestal. From the first optimism a few years earlier there were now several who had given up the dream of a Norwegian oil adventure. After several dry wells and failed attempts to find the black gold under the Norwegian seabed, Phillips was ready to thank him. Had it not been for the certainty that they had to pay the state a million dollars to get out of the drilling program they were obliged to implement, this last well would probably never have been drilled. And everything might be very different today. The find that got Ed Seabourn to scribble the message about Norwegian pedestal as an oil pool was going to be Ekofisk, and it was going to be a little bit bigger than even the greatest optimists in the 1960s could imagine. It's now 46 years since it was found Ecofisk and 44 years since it came into production. It was, and is, one of the biggest findings on Norwegian pedestal ever. Today we will adopt the development of a new Norwegian giant, Johan Sverdrup. I find it interesting to draw the line from Sverdrup to Ekofisk, from one giant to another. The stories of Sverdrup and Ekofisk share many similarities, and they remind us that even in times when you have felt compelled to realize that a dream is over, reality can change in a moment. The blocks that hid both Ekofisk and Johan Sverdrup were both awarded in the first round of the Norwegian concession, in 1965. This year it's been 50 years since this first concession round, and in this way it's amazing that we're going to adopt this very year the expansion of a field that's been hidden in this area ever since. In connection with the 50th anniversary, the Director of the Oil Directorate, Bente Nyland, said: ‘Had the operator Esso had access to the data that existed in 2010, the well could have been located elsewhere – and Johan Sverdrup could then be the first commercial oil find on Norwegian base.’ Johan Sverdrup is located in a mature area east of the Utsira Height. The findings were identified by Lundin in September 2010, following an authorisation granted in 2008 by the TFO. In this way, Johan Sverdrup is a good will on how important the TFO scheme has been and is for development on a Norwegian base. Also in mature areas where you've been able to easily find new elephants with new technology. The Sverdrup field is the largest oil field found on Norwegian base in decades. Not since the 1980s, we've found something similar. When production is on top, the field alone will account for about a quarter of Norwegian oil production. Expected production period is 50 years – in other words, the chance is great that one day my future grandchildren can have a job related to the activity at Sverdrup. At a time when someone is eager to celebrate that the oil age is approaching the end in Norway, I think it is more right to celebrate that we still have many good years of Norwegian oil and gas production in front of us. I would like to thank the case rapporteur, Per Rune Henriksen, for his good and constructive cooperation in dealing with this important matter. With the exception of a few members, the Committee strongly supports a message that Norway continues to have a future as an oil and gas nation. The majority of the committee sees the fantastic opportunities that Sverdrup gives us, in terms of both income and jobs for the future. There are difficult times in the oil and gas industries. I remember well how at the beginning of this great period, while traveling around to get acquainted with the industry, I often heard about how challenging it was with sky-high costs of renting rigging. Now the rigs are in charge, without commission, and the prices have fallen dramatically. For some, it's good news, for many others, it's dramatic. There are many similar examples of how the situation has turned its back on my head since my first day as a big man's representative a year and a half ago. Since then, 22,000 oil jobs have disappeared, and many experience a personal crisis where they are now out of work, but with the same financial obligations as before. The mortgage is to be paid, children are to have leisure activities, and food is to be on the table. It's a tough situation. Fortunately, many quickly find new work, but this does not apply to everyone. That is why I am pleased that the government has amended the law on permitting, so that more people can now avoid dismissal. At the same time, we have increased the number of action places and reduced the threshold into working life by modernising the labour-environment law. These are all important measures in the short term. But short-term solutions do not provide security for the future for those who are now out of work. So as a society, we have to change, create new jobs and give the country more legs to stand on – not because the oil age is over, but because it makes sense in a future picture. If there's one thing the current situation in the oil and gas industry shows us, it's how vulnerable we are when this industry is going through tough times. The most important thing we can do to secure jobs in the oil and gas industry is to facilitate activity and to ensure predictable framework conditions in the industry. It has been facilitated by the government through a record allocation in the TFO and through the 23rd concession round. With the 23rd concession round, the industry has been able to search new areas for the first time since 1994. It offers optimism and opportunities and will provide activity both for oil companies and for the supplier industry. I am also pleased that the majority of the Committee has stated in this position that the framework for the 23rd round of the concession is fixed. At the same time, we work specifically to strengthen Norway's competitiveness. We facilitate the jobs of the future by investing in innovation and new technologies. We're going to spend 1.5 billion. kr more for this now than in 2013, among others. through the Research Council, Innovation Norway and the SketteFUNN scheme. When I attended the oil meeting of the rapporteur in Stavanger, Christine Sagen Helgø, invited a few weeks ago, this effort was brought forward as very important in the future by several participants. We're going to keep the pressure up. At the same time, we have lowered the tax on those who create and invest in Norwegian jobs, in order to secure them for the future. I register that the Workers' Party, at the latest yesterday, believes this shows that we do not understand Norway. But the last thing that a business life under heavy cost pressure needs is 12 billion. kr in tax increases, as proposed by the Workers' Party. There has never been a worse time to increase taxes than now. The development of Johan Sverdrup comes as an important vitamin injection for the oil and gas industry right now. So far, several Norwegian companies have been working on this project, among others. Aker Solutions, Aibel, Kværner Verdal and Kværner Stord, ABB and, no later than Monday, Odfjell Drilling. This ensures jobs for a demanding period of time. I am incredibly proud of the Norwegian supplier industry, which is very internationally competitive, and claim to be in the forefront of competence and quality. The Norwegian oil adventure has been an industrial adventure that has created hundreds of thousands of jobs and local value creation since the beginning of the 1960s. Sverdrup is the largest industrial project in Norway for several decades, and the investments related to the first stage of construction of the field are at 117 billion. kr. Although Norway is in transition and has to prepare for a future in which the value creation of oil and gas will play a slightly less role in the Norwegian economy than it has done in recent years, industry has a bright future. Norwegian oil and gas will help meet the world's energy needs for decades to come. In a world that is going to impose stricter climate requirements, we are well prepared. Our industry already lives well with the world's highest CO2 tax. Several measures have been taken to reduce emissions from the sector and the industry has been working on several fronts to see how they can further reduce their carbon footprint. We can be proud that we have an oil and gas industry in Norway that takes the climate challenge seriously. It will also make Norwegian oil and gas competitive in a greener future, which imposes stricter emission and new technology requirements. Johan Sverdrup will gain power from land from day one of his production period. With it large future emissions have been removed before they even occur. Sverdrup is a future field, an oil field we have every reason to be proud of, for several reasons. We have come a long way since the time Ed Seabourn proclaimed that the North Sea was an oil pool and Ekofisk set the start of the Norwegian oil adventure. Today we adopt the development of a new stage on this adventure. I believe it is appropriate to congratulate Norway on the day, and I look forward to the debate here in the Chamber.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e417	As others have pointed out, this is the largest industrial project in Norway in many decades, and there will be very many jobs in a challenging time for the petroleum sector. The development and operation of the Johan Sverdrup field will generate large revenues for decades to come and many jobs over long periods of operation. In full field development, total investment is expected to be about 200 billion. 2015 crowns, and everyone understands that it gives good opportunities to the equipment industry that so far – and happily – has hijacked very large parts of the contracts for this field. Unlike what we saw just a few years ago, Norwegian suppliers are now competitive, and we have failed to see that the large contracts go to the shipyards in Asia or in Austen. We are very pleased about that, and it shows that change and competitiveness are not something to be taken for granted. We record that the oil and gas industry in Norway is undergoing a process of conversion in which both oil companies and the supplier industry continue to work to strengthen their own competitiveness, reduce costs and increase efficiency. The special feature of this expansion, except for the size of the field, is that the power demand for the Sverdrup field will be met by force from the start of production. It will reduce emissions from this field significantly, but with not very insignificant costs. The petroleum sector is subject to quota and already pays large sums for its emissions, and this is a broad consensus on policy. Due to the size of the Sverdrup find, the field must be extended to several stages of construction. Investment decision and submission of plans for further development and operation are expected in 2017, and planned start of production is in 2022. In the option option, the Progress Party has left a small section that applies to HMS, which is not completely for no reason. One has over time recorded that parts of the HMS work have become a parody that is efficiency limiting and leads to large additional costs. In debates on this issue, the industry has also admitted that there is a single point that challenges the legitimacy of the HMS work and that it is now going through with improvements. That's what it takes. It is also the signal from those added who have this regime around them daily in their work. No one is interested in HMS work being ridiculed and thus loses its meaning for safe and safe operation, so I am pleased with the signals that come to light that this work on a review of the HMS system is being prioritised. Otherwise, the Progress Party supports the requirements of the HMS standard for the award of contracts, in line with the contribution to the case-speaker today. A large part of the configuration debated today by the Committee includes, among other things: information on Norwegian emissions in the oil and gas sector. It is a fact that Norway's extraction of fossil resources is the most climate-efficient available, and is still looking for new cuts. That's good, but when one then asks the square argument that we have to leave large parts of the petroleum resources in the ground, it's a mistake of dimensions. On the day of its possible occurrence, production will be replaced by production in other countries that produce a much higher footprint in terms of climate and environmental conditions. If this is the goal, it simply does not solve the emissions challenges and the climate noise, as it is global. Johan Sverdrup's expansion will also be important for the Norwegian oil and gas industry, as the expansion coincides with a time of low oil prices and lower activity in the industry, and one sees that the Johan Sverdrup field is also important for other established infrastructure by the fact that oil and gas will be transported relatively to Mongstad and Kårstø, where modifications and operations will provide many jobs. The Johan Sverdrup field is one of the largest oilfields in the North Sea, and the field may have a lifetime of more than 50 years, perhaps up to 70–80 years. The decision to expand the field will increase Norwegian production over the years and will, as I said, extend the Norwegian oil age for many decades. I'm very happy about that. This production is needed in the energy mix in the world, as there are many decades before the need for fossil energy will be lower, and our production of this energy is demanded, giving jobs and the economy to our welfare society, and last but not least to the production that contributes at least to the emission of greenhouse gases. There is reason to congratulate all of us today, and thank the case-speaker Per Rune Henriksen for a good job.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e449	I would also like to start by thanking the rapporteur for a good job and also the representative, Bru, for a good cooperation. I also want to be of those who congratulate ourselves on the decision we are going to make on the important extension of Johan Sverdrup today. Oil and gas activities are and will be a pillar of our society. It has contributed to a large and important industrial development, to technological development, and it has helped to develop the whole of society. It is an industry that has had great ring-effects for our whole country, and that will be important in the future. In the Christian People's Party, we have been particularly concerned that we want a larger extraction of oil and gas in existing fields. Because it's about the administration, that's now we have the chance to take it up. The day you shut down the field, it will be demanding and expensive to get started again. We have also been concerned with a further bet on looking for and extracting new fields, but have also been clear that we want a somewhat lower pace than what has been. At the same time, we've been busy protecting particularly vulnerable and valuable areas. It has also proved to be through the cooperation agreement we have with the government, where we have had an important impact on, for example. to protect the areas around the Lofoten, the Vestral and Senja, Jan Mayen, etc. It also appeared in the debate and decisions taken on the opening of the Barents Sea south-east, where the Christian People's Party voted against the opening of the vulnerable areas north of that field. That debate is about the edge of the ice, which will be taken later today. We also believe that health, the environment and safety are important dimensions, also because it is an advantage for Norway. Large parts of the petroleum resources we know need to be located, and then greenhouse gas emissions from the extraction, technology, knowledge and costs will be collected. In order for Norway to be competitive, we must also be at the forefront in this respect. Therefore, power from land is an important key. In a difficult time for the whole industry, the expansion we're going to adopt today means enormously much. We're going through a transition to a low-carbon society, we're going through a green transition. We're going to use the technology workplaces, the competences that humans have, to make the transition we need into a greener society. Forces from countries are essential to reduce Norwegian greenhouse gas emissions. Strength from land is important where it is right, it has been especially important for us to clarify. Because it is not that every field can be electrified, but we believe that Utsira is one of the most suitable areas for it. Therefore, we are also very pleased that the Storting is tightening up the conclusion, decision and condition of today, and state that force from land must be in place for the whole area of Utsira by 2022. We also understand that dimensioning needs to be seen in the context, but connecting the platforms, i.e. Gina Krogh, Edvard Grieg, Ivar Aasen and Johan Sverdrup, is something that can be seen independently of the start-up of construction step 2. The same thing can be done by power cable, because it is fully possible that it can come earlier than the start-up. This is why we believe that there is great sense in seeing the link between the dimensioning and the expansion of phase 2, but at the same time it is clear that force from land for the whole area must be introduced by 2022. I also believe that the important decision we are making today, which we have been working for over a long time, is something that needs to have consequences for new expansions and new upgrades of existing fields, and that it must shape that work by the government. The size says clearly that this is what we think is right – when it is also right in the field in question, when conditions are ready to take effect from land. We are pleased that the Storting makes this decision, that we have put together one – at least – unanimous committee and a unanimous increase – behind the important decision to reduce or prevent more greenhouse gas emissions, and also the principle that force from countries must be the main rule. That has to be the starting point. There are good reasons why it shouldn't be done anywhere, but I'm very happy that we're succeeding in this, and that we're getting a range solution.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e528	SV is voting against the development of Johan Sverdrup and proposes that the development be postponed indefinitely, and we believe that the development is facing a real dilemma between jobs and incomes today and climate change and long-term sustainability. This is a dilemma that several parties should be discussing instead of voting for the development of autopilot. The Johan Sverdrup field is one of the biggest oil fields in Norwegian history, with a lifetime of 50 to 70 years. The decision to expand the field will dramatically increase Norwegian oil production and extend Norwegian oil dependence for several decades. It's a paradox, at a time when world oil use needs to go down in order to combat climate change. Norway must leave oil. Data from the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change show that the oil, coal and gas we have already found contains five to nine times more CO2 than the world can emit if we are to avoid the worst impacts of climate change. Then we have to stop investing in fossil energy and turn investment into renewable solutions. In particular, we need to avoid investments in infrastructure and products and production that lock Norway and the world to large emissions for a long time, as Johan Sverdrup is doing. Norway is a rich country. We've made a lot of money extracting fossil oil and gas over decades. At the same time, we know that the world must let fossil energy lie unused if we are to combat climate change. It is then fair that Norway takes part in this responsibility by leaving the oil in the Johan Sverdrup field. In Norway, we're under a millionth of the world's population, but we're responsible for 2 pst. of the world's oil production and above 3 pst. of the production of gas in the world. In other words, if we are to stop dangerous climate change, we are to get a far greater share than our fair share. If we don't want to reduce production, who's going to do it? We've waited too long for the switch. In Norway, we are expecting an investment rate in the oil industry that we never know could last forever. Decreases in oil prices have contributed to perms and increasing unemployment. We in SV have for many decades warned against a high rate of oil investment, and therefore we have voted against more oil expansions and for the allocation of more land for oil exploration. The big parties in Norwegian politics have wanted a different development. Therefore, the transformation that is now coming will be more challenging, more demanding, but equally necessary. The size response to the fall in an already excessive oil industry cannot be new giant investment in oil. It's not forward-looking. Now, if we are to convert to a greener, sustainable society, lower oil investments will give greater room in the economy to other investments. We must make sure to use the expertise of Norway from the oil age, to develop solutions to the climate problem and to build a greener Norwegian business. For this reason, we will take initiatives towards the Norwegian economy in SV to develop a broad, green migration programme for the Norwegian economy, in order to ensure workers and to use the expertise of the oil age to develop existing and future industries. We have already put forward proposals on the deployment of zero-emission technologies in maritime transport, demonstration installations for sea winds and a major effort to expand rail in the coming years. In our alternative to the revised national budget, we also propose a separate action package on unemployment and on green migration. At a time when it is often heard that all parties can agree on a green shift, a transition to a zero-emission society, it cannot be right when the majority today decides that Norway's largest industrial project in this century will be an oil expansion. Last year, SV took the initiative of an alliance on the Stortinget to ensure that emission-free power is required from land to Johan Sverdrup and other fields on the Utsira Height. Without it, not all of Statoil’s plans for further expansion were based on force from land, and without it, Gina Krog, Ivar Aasen and Edvard Grieg emissions, which together account for 275 000 tonnes, would not have had to shut down gas turbines and use land-based power from 2022 at the latest. Although SV is now in favour of the development of Johan Sverdrup, we are voting in favour of the demands expressed by the majority at Stortinget. Oil and gas industries have increased their emissions by 91 pst. from 1990 until today. The growth of oil industry emissions has been the driving force behind the growth of Norway’s total greenhouse gas emissions and is a main reason why Norway is struggling to achieve the objectives adopted by the Stortinget in climate policy. It doesn't last any longer. The oil industry can no longer be allowed to destroy Norwegian climate targets. That recognition must soon come to others as well. SV will continue to be a driver for climate change requirements for all oil and gas projects, as we have been at the Utsira Height. The Stone Age did not end because of lack of rock, and the fossil age will not end because of lack of fossil resources, but because we have to stop burning them if we are to take care of life on earth somewhat as we know it today. For us in Norway, this means that the oil age must definitely go to the end. The time for major oil expansions in Norway is over. A no to Johan Sverdrup from Stortinget would be a clear signal to Norway and the international community that it is time to speed up the process of conversion, not oil extraction.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e565	Every five years the pope sends out a shepherding letter on how righthearted Catholics should live their lives. Today he has sent out such a letter, in which he apparently calls for an ethical and economic revolution to prevent catastrophic climate change. Such shepherding letters have historically been of great importance. Many people believe, for example: a shepherding letter to improve the social situation of workers in the 1800s speeded up the fight for better conditions and rights. And while the 1800s and 1900s clearly stood in the sign of the labour movement, the overall political perspective of our century is the need to adapt human activity to the limits of the Earth's environment of life. I assume that few representatives use the pope's shepherding letter as a political guide, but it is ironic that the Stortinget is giving a clear signal today to contribute an additional 900 million tonnes of CO2. 18 times Norway’s annual emissions, from the Johan Sverdrup field to global warming. This is a field that's going to produce far beyond the 2070s, and so we're doing this at the same time as we're talking more and more about Norway being a low-carbon society and carbon-neutral in 2050. The Green Party has made it clear that we will vote against the plan for the development and operation of the Johan Sverdrup field. However, a large majority of Stortinget still refuses to lean on a small finger to limit Norway’s sovereign greatest climate impact, namely oil and gas extraction. The decision-making process in oil matters is chemically free of the possibility to assess climate impacts. All decisions have been made, and all investments are locked when the Stortinget finally has a sort of debate about yes or no to opening. Once it is decided that Sverdrup will be expanded, the Environmental Party supports the Green Decision to electrify the field. It is better than nothing, and it contributes to a proper direction in Norwegian energy development. But electrification cutters 1-2 pst. of the greenhouse gas emissions that Sverdrup will produce, and it is almost provocative to hear one representative after another present this measure as a major and decisive contribution to ensuring that the Johan Sverdrup field is not a climate problem. We know, as many people have been asking, that depending on who you ask, 70-90 pst. of the remaining fossil resources of the earth remain in the ground if we're going to have a certain chance to concentrate on two degrees of global warming. Today’s decision presupposes that someone else takes this responsibility, and these others are very much, if not exclusively, countries that would be much more expensive to take this responsibility than it would be for Norway. In addition, the Sverdrup project welds Norwegian economy and business life into the fossil age for even more decades. Sverdrup will link heavy resources, large technology environments and tens of thousands of highly skilled people who should have been thrown into the development of Norway’s green business now. The decision on the Sverdrup field is not comparable to the decision on the Ekofisk field. It was a decision we used to make. The decision on the Sverdrup field is a decision we're going to lose on history. Today, in addition to voting against the development itself, the Greens are going to propose that greenhouse gas emissions from both the production and combustion of petroleum become part of the future plans for expansion on Norwegian soil. We also propose that the power of ocean-based renewable energy should be linked to the area solution, and we are quite surprised that this proposal has not received wider support, because one of the good aspects of the electrification of Sverdrup is that it is now finally establishing an infrastructure that opens up completely new opportunities for the development of liquid sea winds. The size has been dealt with by another proposal from the MDG on a serious seawinding. If there is a real interest in transferring skills and technology from the petroleum industry to future industries, which is so much to talk about, we need to use these kinds of possibilities. Together with other parties, we also promote proposals to carry out a thorough analysis of climate risk in the development phase, and to investigate also the electrification of the Gudrun field and the Sleipner field. Opening the Johan Sverdrup field is a decision across all climate targets, contrary to a fair distribution of the world's remaining carbon budget, and there is a postponement of the green shift in Norwegian business. I hope that the next major industrial decision by Stortinget will be more forward-looking than this.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e734	Petroleum activities have for a long time been one of the carriers of the Norwegian economy. It is Norway’s largest industry measured in value creation in state income and in investment. It will continue to be the petroleum industry for a long time. Development of the Sverdrup field is one of the largest industrial projects in Norway in several decades. This will lead to significant activities in both development and operation, and will also have a good impact by creating jobs both regionally and nationally. But equally important: it will bring great income to the community. The Norwegian petroleum tax system has served Norway well for decades, and the values have benefited the entire Norwegian community, because when some companies are allowed to use the Community's resources, the community has also benefited. This is rather the exception than the rule when looking at countries that have been equally fortunate in finding large petroleum resources. The usual story is that some have earned themselves very rich, while society has stood back with little. In Norway, we chose another route. We demanded that those who were serving to extract the community's resources should pay back. They did, and it will also happen with the Johan Sverdrup field. Only during the first stage of construction is the revenue estimated at about 1000 billion. EUR 2015, and the majority of this is due to the Norwegian society in the form of tax revenue. Nevertheless, recent developments in Norway’s largest industry have been poor. The consequences of cutting and alerting cuts affect and affect many, including outside industry. But what essentially distinguishes this situation from other downfalls in the past is the government's lack of political will to do something to prevent the adverse effects we now see. Although there is broad agreement on the overall objectives and instruments for Norwegian oil and gas activities, we still rely on measures for the present situation. We see that the number of unemployed has increased sharply, and that many of these are from the oil sector. We need action, but obviously the government has no concrete plans to do so. The development of the Johan Sverdrup field has so far awarded major contracts to the Norwegian supplier industry. But no matter how positive it is, we can't let the jubilee over the contract award so far overshadow the fact that we have great challenges to deal with. The supplier industry reports a sudden stoppage in maintenance and investment, leading to mass redundancies. When unemployment rises and there are so many unemployed people directly or indirectly linked to industry, it is noted across the country, for not only the supply industries providing services related to extraction are affected but also more traditional continental industries. This affects many, because after the sale of oil and gas, the supplier industry is our largest export industry. This is an industry that delivers knowledge-based goods and services, not only to Norwegian capital but also to a tough international market. With all their jobs, their value creation and their competence, it's a very important part of our oil wealth as the oil fund itself. We politicians cannot sit here and override the market, but we must equally fully address the challenges we see. We need to listen to those who know the industry and find both long-term and short-term solutions for what benefits society as a whole – and not least: we need to take care of the skills we have, because knowledge is the future, and business will have a central role to play in future value creation, employment and societal development. Even in a low-carbon society, there will be demand for petroleum products, but it is also important that the industry continues to seek new opportunities to reduce its own emissions and to produce lower carbon footprints than they do today. This is important in order to achieve the climate objectives we have set, but also in order to maintain the competitiveness of industry internationally. We need long-term strategies to address the low-carbon society and increase the use of low-carbon technologies. We also need long-term and short-term strategies to maintain Norwegian income and to maintain low unemployment. But then the government and the majority must also show political will to prevent the adverse effects we are now seeing.

In addition, she ridicules the leader of the Workers' Party, who admits that there is a dilemma when at least four fifths of the world's fossil resources have to lie down, and who believes that there is something we have to take over and cannot be unaffected by – that Norway should not at all be concerned with leaving fossil resources behind. So it is a majority of the political parties in Norway who believe that it does not concern Norway at all that much of the world’s fossil resources must remain in place and say instead: ‘Drill, baby, drill!’ No one in the government follows up in the oil policy that we have limitations on greenhouse gas emissions. We need to be aware that the Johan Sverdrup development will lead to an increase in CO2 emissions in Norway due to the production heat they need. And we need to be aware that the climate goals that we in Norway have set ourselves – now the Minister for Climate Change is in the Chamber – are becoming more difficult to achieve, among others. because the electrification of the remainder of the Utsira field is postponed until 2022 at the latest. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e1060	Today, all except the Government Parties vote for the following decisions: « Size sends Report. [[2014-2015]] It is not every day that groceries messages are sent back to the government. This is serious, and I believe that the Minister for Climate and Environment must take over. The Left and Christian People's Party criticise the content of the update, and that's their reason for sending the matter back. The SV and the Greens agree with this criticism, but we also support what is the main criticism of the Greens' report by the Labour Party and the Centre, namely an urgent process of adhoco updating the management plan. The work on management plans has been laboriously structured to provide a framework for the management of our seas. Professional forums, systematic work to cover knowledge gaps and consultation rounds of recordings have been established. This has not been the case with this big message. Through the committee work, we have gained insight into the work on this big message. On 14 January this year, the Ministry of Climate and Environment sends an e-mail to the Norwegian Polar Institute with an urgent order to calculate the ice edge. The bureaucrats of the Minister for Climate and Environment do not ask for technical advice on how to best calculate ice. This is what the Ministry has already decided on. They're just asking the Polar Institute to do the math job. The assignment was sent on 14 January, and the assignment was delivered on 15 January. The map and the 23rd concession round were launched on 20 January. So the ‘professional’ process for this big message took two days, or six days if we're going to be okay. In the opinion of SV, it is not difficult to understand why the government was in a hurry. The government's cooperation agreement with the Christian People's Party and the Left states that oil operations should not be conducted on the ice. The current 2011 management plan has mapped out where the particularly valuable and vulnerable areas are the ice edge and the polar front, and also mapped out the framework for non-petroleum activities. The maps show that several blocks in the 23rd concession round conflict with the ice edge and the polar front. On 6 May this year, Minister for Climate and Environment Tine Sundtoft was asked in the Questionnaire why she felt it was necessary for the Stortinget to move the charted edge of the ice in the management plan. Then she answered: ‘Since the map shows that the ice edge and the north-east parts of the opening areas overlap, there could be a possible conflict between the size of the ice edge. It is the apparent contradiction that has created a need to take a closer look at the ice edge and that was also updated at the announcement of the northernmost blocks in the 23rd concession round. That's what we've now cleaned up using the latest available data for the iceberg." She also said, ‘What was important to us was to look at the apparent contradiction, which it can be interpreted as, by the Storting that one should not start drilling at the edge of the ice at the same time as one has decided where the Barents Sea should be south-east.’ The environmental authorities have also pointed to the conflict in that the government has chosen to highlight blocks in contravention of the current management plan. On 4 April 2014, the Polar Institute writes: ‘One of the proposed blocks northeast of the Barents Sea south-east is directly overlapping with SVO Variable ice edge and blocks 7335/3, 7435/10-12 and 7436/1,10 are either within or in contact with SVO Polarfront. [[7435/9]] In these areas, petroleum activities shall not be commenced in accordance with the Management Plan.» The Norwegian Polar Institute also advises against the allocation of blocks due to the influence of areas with increased ecological vulnerability in the ice edge zone and the polar front. The Committee asked the Minister for Climate and Environment the following questions about the 23rd round of the Polar Institute's observations that blocks in the 23rd round of the concession were in conflict with the current management plan. Then the Minister for Climate and Environment said that the blocks pointed out by the Norwegian Polar Institute are directly overlapping with SVO Variable ice edge and inside or overlapping with the SVO Polar front, as the mapping of the calculation of SVO Variable ice edge and SVO Polar front is done in the Report. St. 10 for 2010–2011 The update of the management plan for the maritime environment in the Barents Sea and the sea areas outside the Lofoten. The conclusion is, then, that the government itself believes that there is a problem with the announcement of blocks in the 23rd concession round and the current management plan being in conflict. The government has a cooperation agreement in which promises have been made to the Christian People's Party and the Left in this area. The government is therefore trying to move the boundaries by updating the calculations for the ice edge of a process that takes two to six days. The result is that the thorough professional processes the management plans are intended to be undermined. When the opinion of the committee became known, changes were made by the government. I quote from an interview with the Minister for Climate and Environment in NRK on 11 June: ‘Do you now have to go to Minister of Oil and Energy Tord Lien and say that part of the blocks announced in the 23rd concession round cannot be allocated anyway?’ Sundtoft answers: ‘No, I believe that the restrictions attached to the 23rd concession round, what we have in our cooperation agreement and the new calculations of the edge of the management plan indicate that this should not cause problems.’ So this is what a climate and environment minister who has just sent his attempt to recalculate the ice edge in return from Stortinget.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e1130	We have management plans for the marine environment for our marine areas. That we should have such plans as tools was presented for the first time in 2002, when the government of Bondevik II presented the big message called ‘Rent and Rich Seas’. Since then, we have had management plans for the Barents Sea and the sea areas outside Lofoten, for the Norwegian Sea and for the North Sea and Skagerrak. We simply have a system in which we lay down the framework for the sustainable use of marine resources and where the precautionary principle is applied. The management plans set out detailed environmental requirements and assess the totality of environmental risks. Norway has a long tradition of creating values from our ocean-based resources. The management plans ensure that this is done in an environmentally sound manner. The Labour Party believes that the knowledge-based management plan regime we have today should be continued. There will be a need for both updates and revisions of our management plans. The management plan for the marine environment in the Barents Sea and the areas around Lofoten was reviewed in the spring of 2006, and we received an update in 2011. A solid professional basis was established for the previous update. There were, among other things, on the basis of a report drawn up by 26 Directorates and research institutions divided into three groups, inter alia. Professional forums. The evidence base was also provided for what is commonly called transparency and observation, and good response was obtained. The update that the government has now provided is not close to being based on a professional basis. Knowledge acquisition has not been carried out on a broad professional basis. Here's the main report, pretty solid stuff -- and it's pretty big, so the referee gets it. And here's the list of the knowledge base, where this main report is also mentioned as part of the subject base, which was on the last page of the paper when the management plan was updated, a long list. In the big message that we're now discussing, the last page is blank, it says nothing. In this case, one element, i.e. the edge of the ice, was presented through the ice data made by the Norwegian Polar Institute on a specific order from the Ministry of Climate and Environment, given on 14 January – which was also referred to by the case-rapporteur – where they specify exactly what data they should have, and what assumptions to base, a kind of formula, really, where it is just filling in. It was obviously not a great job, because on 15 January the department got the answer and the map. So, here the Stortinget has been presented with a case where we are asked to evaluate a map and a calculation, as I see it. It has been suggested that a comprehensive and in-depth revision of the management plan for each sea area should take place every 15 years. In the government’s view, an update of a management plan is more limited than a revision and related to more defined issues, up-to-date knowledge or more demarcated geographical areas. Updates of the plans help to ensure appropriate management frameworks between the overall revisions. Report. Paragraph 20 is such an update. The professional basis for this revision will be developed through a comprehensive process of broad anchoring in the Professional Forum and the Monitoring Group. Work will start in the current accumulation period. Such a centre should be established under the auspices of the United Nations and financed jointly by the United Nations Member States. Such a system could provide protection for people with a real need, while people with other motives could be rejected. At the same time, national treatment of asylum applications will cease. Such a system will be able to restore the sustainability of the asylum institution while enhancing the understanding and acceptance of refugees in the country of destination of the world.

The answer to this is, as I can understand, unfortunately no. The serious thing is that the core issue in all preparedness work after 22 July is the ability to learn lessons from the experience, exercises, supervision and controls carried out by the National Audit Office. The Workers' Party believes that both the government of Stoltenberg and the government of Solberg have shown the ability to learn. I think both governments have the power to do it. We do not in any way deny the efforts that Anders Anundsen has made. We recognise it, just as we recognize the work that Grete Faremo did as Minister of Justice. This does not happen to the Right, the Advance Party and the Left, who in the vote believe that everything that has happened to improvements in the Ministry of Justice and Defence has happened in Anders Anundsen’s time as Minister of Justice – and only then. There, of course, our paths are separated. This is not just historyless, it is politically and intellectually unreasonable. When Grete Faremo became Minister of Justice and Defence on 11 November 2011, she began with a clear mandate to improve preparedness. On 22 July, an administration in the Ministry of Justice left on a bar and in great sorrow. Working conditions have been Spartan since the Ministry of Justice was literally bombed. Nevertheless, 1,046 measures were taken. State Health Authority, as at kgl. Res. On 21 June 2013, the Ministry of Justice’s work in the field of social security and preparedness was entrusted with oversight, but it was also concluded that a number of measures had been taken by the Ministry, in particular as regards crisis management, as they write in the report. This is in line with the opinions expressed in the hearing of former Minister of Justice Faremo and police director Humlegård respectively, who also highlighted all the measures taken just after 22 July. Minister of Justice Anundsen has followed up this work, there is no doubt about it, but it can, according to my best judgement, not doubt that there are still gaps, as the Riksrevision also points out. In my opinion, the government parties take a great chance if they actually insist on believing that everything is now in the very best condition in terms of preparedness, because it is such that if nothing is wrong, there is nothing better to do either. In addition, it appears as a significant paradox that the government parties, which consider the Riksrevision report not to be taken into account, are very concerned that the parts of the Riksrevision report dealing with the former Minister of Justice Faremo’s period as Minister of Justice are valid — but the rest of the report should not be valid. It is a great paradox that I believe this debate will clarify. In the National Audit Office’s study, the preparedness work is being criticised. Coordination is proving problematic in many areas of society. In the field of preparedness, coordination is essential when a crisis situation arises. One of the main findings of the Gjørv Commission was that what went wrong on 22 July, for a large part, was a lack of coordination – about the good forces that did not find each other. Another important finding was just a lack of ability to learn from events and exercises. Against this background, I believe it is very important that the Riksrevision chose to investigate whether these deficiencies were rectified almost four years after the largest terrorist attack on Norwegian soil. The National Audit Office’s investigation is consistent with and draws conclusions in the light of the oversight carried out in the Ministry of Justice. The report of the State Health Authority, presented in April 2014, points out in particular that cooperation between the Rescue and Emergency Department and the Police Department of the Ministry of Justice has been difficult for a number of years and appears to be so demanding that the effectiveness and quality of the work on social security and preparedness can be affected. In the hearing, of course, former Justice Minister Faremo asked what she had done to correct this internal disagreement. She replied that, among other things, obtained external assistance, replaced the department's top management and started work on defining common sense and structure. We recognise Faremo's efforts to strengthen the coordination role in the Ministry of Justice and Defence, but we nevertheless believe that in his two years, Faremo should have moved further in the process of creating a better management culture and thus better interaction within the ministry. We mean both. At the same time, we believe that the Minister of Justice, Anundsen, at an earlier stage, should have made sure that the poor cooperation between the rescue and emergency services and the police department was resolved. A Minister of Justice from a government that has placed great emphasis on repatriation of the effectiveness of preparedness should have given priority to examining what should be done in the coordination of preparedness work as of today. I would like to point out that, several times during the hearing, former Minister of Justice Faremo stated that the core challenge of good coordination both within the Department and with other departments and agencies is that preparedness goes over several levels of government, that it is a limited institutional responsibility, and that it is an administratively managed operational system. Together with the Swedish Environment Party and the Greens, we agree with this consideration and believe that the necessary changes in management should be assessed in such a way as to enable the Ministry of Justice and Defence to monitor effectively its coordination responsibilities. I would also like to point out that the Minister of Justice stressed in the hearing that, soon after the change of government, he started an organisational development process to improve the ministry's work in the field of social security and preparedness, inter alia. in view of the challenges raised by previous supervision, an internal report by the Ministry of Justice and Defence and its own experience. The National Audit Office also writes this in its report and believes that it is too early to say how these changes will turn out. For my part, I hope that the changes that have now been made by Anundsen to improve coordination both within the Ministry of Justice and Defence and the coordination role in other areas will work as intended. The main findings of the Riksrevision study show that there are weaknesses in the management and follow-up of the DSB by the Ministry of Justice and Defence. The Ministry’s governance has been largely activity-based until 2015, and the relationship between activity requirements and overall objectives has been unclear. The reporting of resource use is requested to a small extent and views on the dialogue between the Ministry and the DSB are quite different. I have already explained the disagreement expressed during the hearing and expressed concerns about learning ability if there is no ability to take up criticism as a result of the verification. When I reread the hearing with Director Lea in connection with the processing of the Gjørv Commission report, I was not reassured. Also, Director Lea claimed that since the 2002 review, the department’s oversight had improved and that the DSB and the Ministry of Justice had actively followed up the inspections carried out. Without supervision, Lea asserted that, as it is now, the focus on preparedness in the departments would have been lower, and that, in DSB’s opinion, it was better than in the past. And then, in 2012, at committee head Anundsen's question about how the Ministry of Justice took responsibility for following up exercises, Lea – at the time – replied that he too seemed to be doing this in a better way than before. In other words, it is difficult for anyone to capture that something is wrong and what should be done better when the advice of the supervisory authority is such, and it shows in my view how important it is for independent control, as carried out by the National Audit Office, to be carried out. The National Audit Office’s report stresses that the State plays an important role in promoting systematic and coordinated social security and preparedness work at regional and local level. The county is also linked between central and local authorities. However, it appears that instructions and guidance signals are unclear as to how the co-ordination role of the statesmen should be exercised when events occur. This leads to significant differences in the way jobs both understand and carry out their coordination role regionally. Finally, the Labour Party believes that the Riksrevision has done a thorough and important job when examining the work of the Ministry of Justice and Defence on social security and preparedness. We take the findings of the deepest seriousness, we have a hope that the Minister of Justice is constantly looking for things that can be improved, and we are very pleased that the Stortinget will once again have the opportunity to discuss social security in an overall perspective when the government makes its announcement in 2016. I refer to the vote on the motion for a resolution.
