ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e2154	Work on social security and preparedness is about protecting society from events that could threaten fundamental values and functions and endanger life and health. This is also included in the Message. OJ L 347, 20.12.2013, p. 671. A good preparedness is a prerequisite for people and businesses in our country to feel safe from threatening events and that they can feel that their security is being safeguarded. One must be able to claim that the main task of the State is to ensure the security of its citizens. For this reason, it is important that a continuous focus is on social security and preparedness work, and not least that one carries out regular checks on how this work works in practice on a daily basis. In this way, the Riksrevision report is a good report, and I give tribute to the Riksrevision for this work. The aim of the National Audit Office’s investigation has been to examine to what extent the Ministry of Justice and Defence and the DSB are taking responsibility for the work on social security and preparedness in an effective manner and in accordance with the decisions and assumptions of the Storting. To illustrate this, consideration is given to the exploitation of a range of coordination instruments, including the supervision of departments, the learning of events and exercises and the management and follow-up of emergency personnel. The survey was mainly based on the period after 22 July 2011 and the data collection was completed in November 2014. The National Audit Office’s audit revealed the following: Serious weaknesses in the exercise of coordination responsibilities weaken societal security and preparedness. Three out of nine ministries have little followed up previous supervisory recommendations by the DSB on behalf of the Ministry of Justice and Defence. The administration and follow-up of emergency services by the Ministry of Justice and Defence and the emergency services of the counties is deficient. No systematic evaluation and follow-up of exercises and events is carried out. There are weaknesses in the management and follow-up of the DSB by the Ministry of Justice and Security. The DSB’s activity management contributes to a limited extent to good target and performance. The National Audit Office’s report mainly concerns governance between administrative bodies and not the areas directly related to the operational part of preparedness. Nevertheless, the Riksrevision concludes that ‘the preparedness in Norway is about the same level as after 22 July’, that the preparedness is so ‘deficient that Norway’s preparedness is weakened’ and that ‘Norway is a less secure country than it should have been’. These quotations are taken from the National Press Conference of 21 May 2015, the National Press Conference of NRKs, the Post of Aften and VG respectively. On the basis of the impression of the seriousness of the Riksrevision report and the opinions of the National Auditor on the submission of the report, the Committee concluded that there was a need for consultation calling for key actors in social security and preparedness. As for what is part of the social security and preparedness work, I would like to quote what Director Jon A. Lea of the DSB said in his opening lecture: ‘The preparedness in Norway can be divided into four pillars, or four categories: first, the defence and second, the police’s work on crime — these two tasks are not the subject of this report. So it's life and health, which is handled by the emergency situations for health, fire and police, HRS, the volunteer organizations, the civil defence and others. That is not the subject of this report. And then there are societal security challenges beyond that, like, for example. electricity supply, eco-supply, transport, industry, extreme weather, floods, pandemics and radiation protection. The national audit review has concentrated on the management and control regime within a selected part within the last category.» From this point of view, the Progress Party considers that the Riksrevision report cannot be used as a phase of the current situation, but must be seen as a report that points to possible weaknesses in a limited part of the emergency area. The Progress Party has noted that all participants in the oral hearing of the Control and Constitutional Committee agreed that the Riksrevision has provided a good review of the parts of the coordination responsibility covered by the report. Furthermore, the Progress Party believes that this is a good foundation for further work, even though we disagree with the conclusions of the National Audit Office in some areas, and we also believe that there is no basis for drawing such far-reaching general conclusions that have been made on the basis of the National Audit Office's investigations. The Progress Party points out that the Riksrevision report is based mainly on the carried out supervision of the Health Authority, which was concluded in December 2013. Upon the explicit request of the Control and Constitutional Committee during the hearing of Director Jan Fredrik Andresen of the State Health Authority, it was black that no additional figures, facts or assessments were provided by the Health Authority to the National Audit Office after the conclusion of the Health Authority report. Thus, the management report largely reflects the efforts to improve preparedness measures as they faded at the end of 2013. This also appears to be confirmed by the National Audit Office’s reference to the Health Authority’s report for its part. The Health Authority found that the cooperation relations between the Police Department and the Emergency and Rescue Department in the Ministry of Justice and Security were so difficult that this could affect social security and preparedness work, and that this was also a matter that had been known for a long time and which was pointed out by previous supervision. When Minister of Justice and Defence Grete Faremo joined, there was a clear mandate to improve preparedness. The Party of Advancement refers to the former Council of State Grete Faremo said during the hearing: ‘I started as Minister of Justice and Security on 11 November 2011 with a clear mandate to improve preparedness. Good preparedness conditions, in my opinion, clear and good leaders and a structure that can be managed. Culture for management and governance had to be strengthened in the Ministry of Justice and in underlying agencies. You do not adopt good management, you have to work it out, with demands for managers, new skills, modern work processes and interaction.’ The Progress Party considers it critical that the Council of State did not adequately address the cooperation problems encountered in the Ministry of Justice and Defence after she took up the position. These were problems that have been known for a long time, and as it was absolutely necessary to get solved. As it appeared during the hearing, this created problems for the underlying agencies. After the accession of the current Minister of Justice, Anders Anundsen, this issue was really addressed shortly after the government change, by setting up an organisational development process to improve the ministry's work in the field of social security and preparedness, among other things. in view of the challenges identified in previous supervisory review, on the basis of an internal report of the Ministry of Justice and Defence from 2012 onwards and on its own experience. The Progress Party refers to the reorganisation of the Ministry of Justice and Security from 1 January 2015 with a crisis management, preparedness and security department and a department dealing with prevention security and analysis. These are measures such as: shall reply to the observations made in the Health Authority’s report. I find it reassuring that this very area has now been taken into account and resolved by the Council of State Anundsen, as there is a prerequisite for good cooperation between important departments to deal with security and emergency issues. This was also confirmed by police director Odd Reidar Hoplegård, where he initially spoke at the hearing: ‘Changes in the Ministry’s internal organisation of social security and preparedness have resulted in simpler governance and a more holistic management of this important social area.’ The current government has changed previous practice so that all supervisory reports on social security and preparedness work in the various departments are presented to the government for discussion. In addition, separate government emergency meetings are held and frequent meetings are held in the Government Security Committee. I also refer to the State Council of Anders Anundsen said during the hearing that: ‘the government is anchoring all these supervision in the government as such, both when the supervisory reports are available and when the follow-up is completed, so that the government is aware of what has happened and has not taken action. There is an important strength for the ability of the Ministry of Justice and Security to influence the work of civil society security and preparedness in other departments.’ This is also important to give legitimacy to the effective supervision of DSB as a directorate of a department in other departments, as the Director of DSB also pointed out during the hearing. The progress batch has also noted that DSB has been more involved in the follow-up work than before, cf. DSB Director Jon A. Lea said: «The DSB is now also given a greater role in the follow-up to the supervision of the individual departments.» With regard to the management and follow-up of the county councillors, the Progress Party agrees with the Riksrevision that there should be better consistency between the governance signals from the central authorities and the resources made available for the emergency work of the county councillors. I also refer to DSB Director Jon A. Lea's posts during the hearing where he said that: ‘The Ministry should give greater priority to the efforts and follow-up of the county council members and considers that there is a lack of consistency between professional expectations, increasing portfolios of tasks and the financial framework allocated to the county council members’. I refer here to the State of Sogn and Fjordane, Anne Karin Hamre, said during the hearing that ‘the management has improved in recent years, but resources are a bigger problem than lack of control’. The party of progress assumes that the government will follow this up and refer to the State Council of Anders Anundsen at the hearing that a ‘new State Instructions to clarify the responsibilities of state officials. It, combined with the project Better governance of the State, where one will go through the portfolio of tasks and funding, is intended to contribute to addressing some of the challenges that the county councillors correctly point to.» The Progress Party will also highlight the development of new guidance for the work of local authorities on social security in order to make the social security work of the counties more holistic and systematic and will at the same time refer to the Director Lea of the DSB, who said about the good work being done by the counties towards the municipalities today. The report of the National Audit Office and the Health Inspectorate report show that the governance dialogue between the Ministry of Justice and Security and the DSB and the Directorate of Police is deficient. I would like to point out here that during the hearing, DSB said that the governance dialogue between the Ministry of Justice and the DSB had at times been strenuous, but that it is much better today, and that the dialogue with the current Council of State is good. Similarly, police director Humlegård commented on this during the hearing, and I quote from his comment: ‘The target structure has been simplified, and changes have also been made in the department, reduced from three departments to two, so that we are now dealing with the police department, with which we have an excellent dialogue.’ The organisation of the Prime Minister's office has been taken up by the Committee, although the Riksrevision report does not concern the government. The Progress Party refers to the fact that the emergency structure of the SMK does not have a decisive role in crisis management and does not change the decision-making structure established. The Ministry of Justice and Defence is the Permanent Ministry of Management, unless a different Ministry of Management is appointed. The crisis support unit of the Ministry of Justice and Defence shall support the Ministry of leadership and the Council of Crisis in their work. The crisis support unit also employs the civil situation centre, which cooperates closely with the Ministry of Foreign Affairs operational centre and the situation centre of the Ministry of Defence/Defence. Following the accession of the new government, a number of measures have been implemented to improve social security and preparedness in our country, and together with the measures implemented by the former government, more than 1000 different measures have been registered. There won't be enough time to mention all of this here, but I just refer to the selected actions that were mentioned earlier by the representative Tetzschner. As I said at the outset, the Riksrevision has given the impression that the preparedness is no better today than it was after 22 July 2011. So we have to ask ourselves, is that true? In the light of the hearing, I would argue that there is no basis for the far-reaching conclusion reached by the Riksrevision. The Riksrevision has given many good impressions to the Stortinget about things that one should continue to work with, and I have great confidence that the Minister of Justice will take care of this in the future. However, the main impression today must be that preparedness is better today than it was on 22 July 2011. This has also been confirmed by statements by Director Lea of the DSB and police director Humlegård, and not least by Minister of Justice Anders Anundsen, who confirmed the statements made by Lea and Humlegård on this point. We are so absolutely on the right track in this important work. But some remain, and we will probably never be completely finished either, because changing times require constant changes in strategies and emergency procedures after the current situations at all times. This is such an important matter that social security and preparedness should be discussed in the full range of the Storting, both in the light of the findings of the studies carried out by the Health and National Audit Office, and in the light of the updates we have now received through the Committee's consultation on the matter. The Progress Party here points out that State Council Anders Anundsen announced during the hearing that the government intends to present a new report on social security during 2016. This is met by the Progress Party, so that the Storting can just discuss the preparedness and security work in an overall context. Furthermore, the Communication should also address management, culture and attitudes, i.e. operational management. In addition, the notification should discuss how the oversight of departments and underlying agencies can contribute to strengthening preparedness. The relationship between the Directorate for Social Security and Preparedness and the County and Municipalities should also be mentioned.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e2244	The independent Commission, the Gjørv Commission, appointed by the government of Stoltenberg in August 2011, was commissioned to review and draw lessons from the attack on the government quarter and the massacre on Utøya on 22 July 2011. The Commission’s conclusions set out five assumptions as key reasons for the failure of this fateful day. Among other things, it is mentioned that the ability to recognise risks and take lessons from practice was too small, the ability to coordinate and interact has been inadequate, management's ability and willingness to clarify responsibilities, establish goals and take action to achieve results has been inadequate. The National Audit Office’s survey, based on the questions of what we have learned, and what has been improved by general preparedness and social security since 22 July 2011, will therefore be followed up by key issues relating to the evaluation of 22 July. These are two questions about the very basis for people's sense of security, and what kind of expectations the individual can have for the big community when terrorist attacks or other similar disasters occur. The study has focused on the extent to which the Ministry of Justice and Security and the Directorate of Social Security and Preparedness take effective care of their responsibilities for the work in this area and not least in accordance with the decisions and assumptions of the Storting. In particular, it was chosen to focus on a selection of coordination instruments, including the monitoring of departments, the learning of events and exercises and the management of the emergency response forces for the period 22 July 2011 to November 2014. In addition, the Health Authority report, which was delivered in April 2014, is a central part of the basis for the report of the Riksrevision. The National Audit Office’s report shows that serious weaknesses in the Ministry of Justice’s coordination and governance weaken the work on social security and preparedness in general. Three ministries, including the Ministry of Justice, have little followed up on post-supervisory recommendations, limited learning of exercises and events, and weak management and follow-up of county emergency services. This, in the view of the Christian People's Party, is a serious sentence on the central aspects of the security of preparedness and society in our society. No measurable improvements have been made in key areas that were perceived as the main cause of the failure on 22 July. This is serious. The interpretation of the conclusions of the study has revealed different views on the main findings of the Riksrevision. Is this to be seen as a description of a deterioration in overall preparedness and social security during the period, or is it a description of a part of the preparedness? While a central part is appropriate, it is not a description that provides a basis for drawing conclusions on the whole. The receipt of the report by the various parties involved has been clearly characterised by differing views on how the report should be read and understood. It is especially when a central actor in the overall emergency response work, DSB, by Director Lea, believes that: ‘The conclusion of the Court of Auditors creates a false impression of the status of the Norwegian preparedness. I cannot see how the Riksrevision has come to its conclusion on the basis of this report.’ So it's going to be added that the same person also expressed that ‘The Court of Auditors has carried out a good review of important parts of the coordination responsibility of the Ministry of Justice and Defence. In many areas, there is much to learn.’ Nevertheless, there is an impression that an important and central directorate with a high level of responsibility for the daily preparedness in Norway has chosen to distance itself from the criticisms of the Riksrevision and its conclusions. In particular, the director of the DSB makes such a strong criticism of the work of the National Audit Office. Here, in our view, DSB should have taken a more humble and learning attitude than what one experienced during the hearing. The Council of State also did not share DSB’s assessments as it appeared during the hearing, although his opinions in the reply to the Riksrevision could be perceived in a slightly different way. The main impression of the hearing is that the Council of State shares the Riksrevision’s conclusions that during the Riksrevision period, there were serious weaknesses in the use of the coordination instruments, and that the division of roles and responsibilities between the directorate and the ministry in some areas appeared unclear, which weakens efficiency and reduces the quality of social security work, and that is what is serious in the matter. It seems strange that the reality of the functioning of coordination, as shown in the Riksrevision report, is so different between the department and the directorate, and it is critical that this was not resolved at an earlier time. According to the Christian People's Party's assessment of the report and the hearing, there are good reasons to say that the dialogue between the ministry and the DSB, which had been experienced in the past, was strenuous until about a year ago. One year ago, from the DSB side, it is now perceived as good by both parties. There is also no basis for claiming that today there seem to be different perceptions of communication between the DSB and the ministry. Some of the main findings of the Gjørv Commission were that what went wrong on 22 July 2011 was largely a lack of coordination as well as a lack of ability to learn from events and exercises. Coordination we know is challenging in a number of areas of society. Working across departments and sectors that initially have close shoots between businesses requires practice and conscious attitudes about when and how to cooperate. At the same time, we know that good coordination is crucial when crises arise. In the opinion of the Christian People's Party, therefore, the criticism of the preparedness work becomes serious when it is concluded from the Riksrevision that the Ministry has not ensured that the sector as a whole has targeted and effective preparedness work, not a sufficient overview of the status and state of the civil sector social security work and a lack of overview of exercise activities based on ministry reporting. The Ministry of Justice was particularly hard hit on 22 July 2011. Therefore, understanding must be shown that in such circumstances, one had limited capacity in the early days. It was recognised, both in the report of the State Health Authority, from the National Audit Office and in the hearing, that there were clear challenges in the Ministry of Justice and in the coordination of underlying agencies. In particular, there is a need to highlight the conflicts of competence between the two departments, the rescue and preparedness department and the police department. There is reason to believe that this conflict had a negative impact on the possibilities for functional and good coordination of preparedness work in the Ministry of Justice and the underlying agencies. Yet, having such a paralysing conflict over time is unacceptable, and there is a clear political responsibility on its part at all times to resolve that kind of conflict. The conflict was recognised by all the Councils during the hearing and described as a challenging and demanding situation with which everyone was working to find a solution. At the same time, we must be able to state that, despite that recognition, the problems in this area persisted even after the government change in the autumn of 2013. That is why it is critical, as we see it, that former State Council Faremo, which was responsible for political affairs during this period, did not take the necessary steps to resolve this conflict. It must be said that, during the hearing, former State Council Faremo did not contribute in a particularly constructive manner to informing the matter. There was little willingness for self-recognition, little willingness to point out what could have been done differently, and it's in itself very worrying."For the Committee, for the Biggest and for all those who are going to contribute to creating security in society, it's absolutely essential that we learn. Then we have to have experience on the table, achievement has to be sidelined, and it has to be recognised what are weaknesses in the system. Then first we can learn, then we can make improvements. It is unfortunate and critical from our point of view that the State Council of Faremo did not make an adequate and constructive contribution to informing the matter. We're not looking for scapegoats, but we're looking for improvement points, learning points, so that the safety of our inhabitants is as good as possible. It was therefore necessary for the present State Council Anundsen to address this devastating conflict in order to resolve it. By acquiring external competences and new organisational approaches, things may indicate that the conflict is now being resolved and that an important cause of lack of coordination and coordination is no longer existing in the same way as in the past. That work is to be done by the Council of State Anundsen. In addition to this, a number of new measures have been launched to strengthen preparedness work, many of which were initiated by the Councils of State in the Red-Green Government and continued and further developed by the Council of State Anundsen. It is worth noting that after 22 July 2011, there has been a continuous effort to improve preparedness in many areas. The hearing has shown that one is well underway in several of these areas, but there are still weaknesses in several of the areas identified by the Riksrevision. One of the areas that appeared to be demanding is the lack of development of the target and performance management system. The control parameters are designed to a small extent as performance requirements. Instead, governance is based on requirements to carry out specific activities. Instead of telling what qualitative results to achieve, a previous expectation was expressed by the Ministry of Justice of the DSB of the activities to be carried out and their number. Individual activities became a measure in themselves, moving attention away from target and result attainment to only the quantifiable sizes. The report by the State Health Authority assesses the target and results system in the Ministry of Justice as poorly developed, as formulated. The objectives, as they have worked up to now, bear the mark of being visionary or theoretical and lack a design that can make them sufficiently concrete and measurable, with qualitative content and not just a quantification of activities. It is not difficult to see this issue in the light of the previously mentioned conflict between the central departments responsible for preparedness and social security in the Ministry of Justice. When the department does not have a unified and coherent strategy, the objectives and management signals will appear to be diffuse. To map the target achievement in the field of emergency response, we understand that we are demanding. At the same time, it may also be that where there are no clear objectives, it will be difficult to get two departments to work in the same direction. A clearly operationalised target structure will be a central mechanism for alleviating disagreement between the two departments of the Ministry of Justice. In addition, it would guide what is expected in the underlying emergency chain departments. It is therefore interesting that the Council of State, Anundsen, initiated a work that had just planned to review the whole target structure across the justice and preparedness sectors, including social security and preparedness, where all the departments were involved. The Council of State said, as I understood it – perhaps we can get it confirmed by the Council of State even later today – during the hearing that from the current year a completely new and different common target structure has been put in place. In this area, there must be a target system that is clear and concrete, but that is dynamic at the same time, in order to adapt to the reality that we know is constantly changing in the field of preparedness. The National Audit Office’s report underlines the importance of the State as a driving force and coordinating body for social and security work at regional and local level. At the same time, the National Audit Office’s study shows a mismatch between the professional expectations and the management signals given by DSB, in conjunction with the resource frameworks made available by the Ministry of Justice. From 2003 to 2013, the number of annual works has been reduced by 30 p.m., from 4,5 annual works to 3,2 annual works at each post. At the same time, where it is emphasised that the mandate of county officials contains few measurable performance requirements, there is limited opportunity to assess the contribution of county officials to achieving the objectives in the field of preparedness. Inadequate coordination at regional level creates a risk of a reduction in national enforcement capacity if the serious events occur. It is therefore positive that the Minister of Justice informed during the hearing that action has been taken to strengthen this work, under the authority of the county council. There is no doubt that this important part of social security and preparedness work should also be given more resources in the future, not just new tasks. The police reform, with the disaggregation of new regions not following the borders, will further create challenges for future coordination and coordination of county response tasks, which will probably require further new resources. A significant part of the improvement work in the field of preparedness and safety is the implementation and follow-up of supervision. While previous practice has been that the supervision of each department has been a matter for the department in cooperation with the Ministry of Justice, this is now being presented to the government for discussion. No deviations are closed until a breach of requirements has been corrected. This is good. It is also positive that supervision will now be public. It provides insight into what has been done and what is still waiting for action. The National Audit Office criticises the lack of follow-up and evaluation of exercises and events. It is positive that a new framework for the evaluation of this type of activity is now being set up, and there is reason to respond to the outcome of this work and what it may mean for the strengthening of preparedness. On the part of the Christian People's Party, we are concerned about the serious findings pointed out by the National Audit Office in its report. These are key aspects of preparedness that require maximum attention in the future. At the same time, it is also necessary to point out that the report is not a phase for the whole emergency situation. There is an investigation which has produced serious deficiencies on important aspects, but nevertheless within a defined area of emergency response. There has been ongoing work on improving preparedness in several areas since 22 July 2011. The hearing has shown that one is well-informed in some areas, but still weak in key areas. In summary, it seems that a new approach has been taken to the preparedness work following the government change in autumn 2013 in several areas, among others. a better dialogue between the Department and the DSB, organisational action that has helped to resolve the conflict in the Ministry of Justice, to open up and strengthen the follow-up of supervision and a clearer and more concrete target structure for the entire preparedness process. There is still a lack of improvement in the former Ministry of Agriculture and Food and the Ministry of Fisheries and Coastal Affairs. Here we expect that there will be a close follow-up in the continuation. The most serious one in my time, said Foss. He went on to say: ‘After the Gjørv Commission presented its proposals for improvements in the autumn of 2011, we see no significant improvement capacity in the department.’ This is a serious statement. The Ministry of Justice and Defence is entrusted with a coordinating role in the area of preparedness and the following key areas are addressed in the report: Serious weaknesses in the exercise of coordination responsibilities weaken societal security and preparedness. There are weaknesses in the management and follow-up of the DSB by the Ministry of Justice and Security. The DSB’s activity management contributes to a limited extent to good target and performance. That is the basis on which the Committee has examined this matter. The aim of the National Audit Office’s investigation has been to identify the extent to which the Ministry of Justice and Defence and the DSB are responsible for the work on social security and preparedness in accordance with the decisions and assumptions of the Storting. According to standard audit practice, the conclusion of the National Audit Office is based on a review of management documents and other written material as well as some interviews. The conclusions are based on this work and not on how the preparedness would actually work on a real event. The last thing I want to underline is as strongly as I can. The result may be the opposite, with less readiness because too much energy is going to follow the rules.’ The audit firm PwC carried out an independent evaluation of the major fires at the request of the State Council and Anders Anundsen. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e2455	Normally we tend to start by thanking the case rapporteur, but I have some critical comments on the case rapporteur that I am going to get to, so it is possible that I can thank the case rapporteur for the next round, when he may have clarified the points I have. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e2648	The Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs of the European Parliament has a very important role to play. It shall perform in a thorough and credible manner the essential part of the direct control function of the Storting pursuant to the Constitution. Similarly, the National Audit Office has a similar role, and it has large resources to monitor whether the administration complies with the decisions and assumptions made by the Stortinge. Both bodies possess great power, which often results from important tasks. Therefore, the level of precision is also very important, in terms of both audit criteria, methodology and not least conclusions and presentation of the findings made by the National Audit Office. The purpose of the supervision carried out and addressed to the departments and the purpose of the Riksrevision studies is for the administration to learn to implement changes where required. These are important instruments to improve governance. The basis for this case is the National Audit Office’s investigation of the Ministry of Justice and Defence’s work on social security and preparedness. It is not a public security and preparedness study in general, but, as several of the representatives have been involved, it is confined to a part of the coordinating responsibility of the Ministry of Justice and Defence, in particular through the responsibility for the supervision of other departments, as well as the administration of the DSB and its members and the management of DSB’s operations. An overall committee points out that the Riksrevision report deals with a limited proportion of preparedness work in Norway and that the report is not an exhaustive review of the emergency situation in Norway. I share that view and believe it also reflects what was said by the police director and the head of the Directorate of Social Security and Preparedness in the conducted audit hearing – and to which I joined. For the sake of order, I would also like to point out that I am not repeating to a small extent what I said in the inspection hearing on Saturday 6 June, and will therefore refer to my initial post there, where I addressed the issues raised by the Riksrevision report, and also reviewed some of the measures implemented, or under implementation, which are not covered by the report. Since there has been a discussion in the media on the balance between criticising a national audit report on the one hand and taking lessons on the other, the majority of the Committee considers that the controlled bodies should also take up objections where they consider this to be justified, important. At the same time, there must be no doubt about the will and ability to learn from findings. For things to get better, you have to have the will to recognize faults and shortcomings. I am very concerned that the justice sector should be a learning organisation. Then, of course, we also have to accept that professional disagreements can occur, and that's part of a learning process that includes all of us. I also think it is good when the majority of the committee points out that the Riksrevision conclusions are based on a review of the management documents and other written material as well as some interviews, and not on how the preparedness would work under a real event. I therefore agree with the majority of the Committee that the Riksrevision report cannot be used as a phase in the real situation, but must be seen as a report that points to weaknesses in part of the preparedness work and area. It's important. I also notice that the majority of the committee refers to the fact that, by looking up the media on the same day as the report was presented, and in the next few days, it was made up of the impression that the findings of the report were extraordinary. I therefore think it is very good that an overall committee takes note that after 22 July 2011, there has been a continuous effort to improve preparedness, and that many areas have been successful, although there is still a lot that needs to be improved. Following the comments by the representative Raja on whether we have an optimal preparedness today, I would like to stress in particular that I believe that this is a point we – in a way – never come to, because it will always be a potential for improvement. Indeed, work on social security and preparedness is ongoing over time. This government has launched a wide range of entirely new and concrete measures to improve social security and preparedness. In some areas, we have also continued and reinforced actions undertaken by the previous government. At the same time, I would like to stress that the National Audit Office's study describes important challenges in a complex area. I would like to refer to the hearing, where I pointed out that the findings are important contributions to the further work in my department. These inspections are developed over time and look different today than they did when they were started. As the Committee points out, there has been too much failure in the past to follow the supervisory recommendations, with the result that improvements to the next supervision should be better in some cases. The changes we have now made by strengthening supervision, by making it clear that there are deviations, and that we are not closing supervision until the deviations are closed, that we are anchoring supervisory results and follow-up in the government as such, as well as opening up public oversight results, I believe that they will be effective, and I am pleased that the Committee agrees. I also think it is good that the Committee expresses satisfaction with the work we have been doing to ensure the follow-up of learning points in events and exercises, for example by using some of the same instruments that we are now using, after we have revised the supervision and instruments in the follow-up to supervision. The Committee points out that the Riksrevision criticises the Ministry's management and follow-up of the DSB. I would therefore like to remind you that several changes have been made in the department's management and follow-up of the DSB after the government change. This applies to both management dialogue, new targets and new management parameters. The aim has been to simplify and improve the management of the underlying establishment. I would also like to remind you of what I said in the hearing, that the development of targets and not least control parameters is also a complex task that needs to be solved over time, and that needs to be changed over time. But our aim has been to get a new structure, which is to simplify and improve communication and management of the underlying establishment. It's a work that we've now done quite a lot of changes, but we still have to work on to make this system get better and better, and it has to be done in cooperation with the underlying agencies. This applies not only to the DSB, but also to other underlying agencies in the Ministry of Justice and Defence. The majority in the committee, in a note, have asked the government to provide enough resources for the statesmen so that they can carry out the preparedness work in a satisfactory way. The number of years spent on standby in the State has been reduced in recent years. I'm just mentioning it: in the North-Trande County, there's only two and a half year's work to be done on standby. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e3075	The National Audit Office has submitted a complete notification of its activities in 2014. I present a unanimous opinion from the Control and Constitutional Committee on the subject of the message. It is the opinion of the Committee that the message well describes the work and activities of the National Audit Office in 2014. The Committee is pleased that planned actions for 2014 were mainly implemented in line with the business plan. Both this and previous messages show that the work of the National Audit Office is absolutely essential for the Storting to be able to carry out its control of government and administration in a good way. The National Audit Service writes in the message that there is a need for continuous improvement of processes and systems in order to succeed in its mission. It is reassuring that the biggest external control body in Storting focuses on this. The national audit carries out user surveys for the different types of audits each year. It is positive that the Riksrevision thus obtains feedback from audited establishments and departments. The Committee has noted that the user surveys generally provide good results – this applies to all types of audits. It bears witness to the fact that the Riksrevision is an organisation that has a great deal of respect for the administration. There's a lot of evidence that there's a good interaction between the National Audit Office and the government. However, there are some challenges that I would like to point out from my point of view. It is not always the government that follows up what is pointed out by the National Audit Office. It happens too often that the Riksrevision has to point out the same weaknesses over and over again. It is therefore important that administrations learn from the work of the National Audit Office, including accounting audit, company control and administrative audit. Discussions in the wake of the National Audit Office’s work are important for the development of good and efficient administration in Norway. It is perhaps the administrative audit that is most often the subject of discussion. It is also possible that the members of the Control and Constitutional Committee may have different opinions on the details of the administrative audit. It is essential to remember that this is also an expression that the National Audit Office focuses on current and important policy areas through the administrative audits. We should be grateful for that. The Riksrevision was reorganised on 1 May 2014. The Committee hopes that the reorganisation can contribute to the good development of the organisation. The national audit represents a high level of professional competence, including international competence, and it is important that the strong environment of competence is maintained and further developed. Finally, I would like to say a little bit about the National Audit Office's international work and activities. Norway has a strong national audit — also internationally. It may not be by chance that the Norwegian National Auditorate is sought as a member of so-called peer reviews, where national audits assess each other’s audit practices and organisation. As an example from the message, I can mention that for the fourth time the Norwegian National Audit Service has participated in a peer review of the US National Audit Office. This is the second time that the Norwegian National Audit Office has conducted this work. In my opinion, it is an expression of high professional quality that other national audits also recognise. The Committee is also pleased that the National Audit Office helps other countries to build up a good State Audit Authority. National audit assistance projects are based on the idea that an independent and effective national audit is a fundamental prerequisite for a well-functioning public sector. It is very important for the fight against corruption. The notification refers to the National Audit Office’s institutional cooperation projects in several countries. The Committee welcomes the fact that the Riksrevision can contribute to an improved system of governance in this way. The Committee agrees with the content of the Communication of the Riksrevision and it is a unanimous committee that submits it as a referral to the Stortinget.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e3127	At the request of the Transport and Communications Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 5 minutes to each party group and 5 minutes to the government. The President will also propose that up to five lines of reply from members of the Government be given within the allocated speaking time and that those who have to sign up to the allocated speaking time be given a speaking time of up to 3 minutes. – It shall be deemed to have been adopted. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e3136	This case is about road safety, and all the parties at Stortinget share the vision of zero fatalities in traffic. A loss of life in traffic is one too much, and then it is good to see that the increase in traffic safety in the majority of the population, such as high growth in road maintenance, several middles, three times as many rumen fields and four times as many controls on heavy vehicles, produces results and that the decrease in the number of fatal accidents continues. This is very good. The Committee as a whole is committed to providing drivers with a safer everyday life. But today the issue is about the use of a specific instrument, namely distance-ATK, or average measurement. Here the points of view are divided. There is no doubt that those who wish to make extensive use of the route ATK have the aim of increasing road safety. I also do not doubt that the route ATK can have a certain effect, but the report should not be read as the only truth, because it has clear weaknesses. TØI itself writes that the data base should have been about five times larger, and we politicians know that when it comes to polls, at least over 1,000 people should be asked to give a real picture. So when TØI writes that it should be five times greater than what has been the basis, it's important to notice that. Furthermore, they say that accident reduction can be a result of coincidences, but that they have nevertheless concluded that the decrease is probably due to distance ATK. I reread the report yesterday, and then contacted TØI and the one who had written the report. She was able to document the causes of the 22 fatal accidents mentioned in the report. I think that's the most important thing in this context. I asked how many of these were due to high speed, and how many were due to other reasons, such as driving in a rush, the road's constitution, suicide, difficult weather conditions, the car's driving technical condition, etc. I was then told that there were at least 2 accidents out of the 22 how high the speed was the main reason – i.e. 2 out of 22 accidents. So the other accidents could have happened regardless of whether it was the distance-ATK or not. She further wrote that in the other accidents was also high speed likely, at least as a contributing factor, but based on the information she had available, she could not exclude other factors, so she would not speculate. To be honest, it's a very thin basis to make a decision if we're going to open up to widespread use of such a controversial tool as the route ATK. In another report, ‘Death accident analysis in road traffic 2012’ from the State Road Administration, the cause of fatal accidents has been analysed. Accidents are often caused by many factors, and in 2012, high speed was a contributing cause of 32 pst. of the fatal accidents. A big difference in weight – in other words, meeting accidents where a car hits a car, a passenger car hits a cyclist or an MC, or how old Lada hits a new SUV – was the reason for 31 pst. of the fatal accidents in 2012, i.e. One-third. The fact that the car is old and does not have modern safety equipment is contributing cause and factor for death in almost half of the fatal accidents, and conditions such as worn tyres, driving in a drug-induced condition, suicide and difficult driving conditions are other reasons. Much of this can never be changed by using the distance ATK, and I think there is a thin basis for calculating when the report from TØI takes all kinds of fatal accidents on the routes, instead of just looking at the number of fatal accidents where speed has been the determining cause. Against this background, I would like the Stortinget to have supported our proposal that it is natural to go through the guidelines and use of the route ATK in connection with the notified traffic safety message. One thing that unfortunately Stortinget does not seem to be concerned about anymore is privacy and monitoring. Now it's true that the images will automatically be deleted if you haven't been driving illegally, but in all cases where the Storting is going to discuss the challenges of monitoring, we need to see it in a larger context. With a few keystrokes, you can easily convert the route-ATK scheme so that the images are not deleted. It won't happen under this government, and it probably won't happen under the next government, but we don't know what society will be like in 10 or 20 years. Then I believe that it would be problematic to organise extensive monitoring of law-abiding citizens, and, for privacy reasons, would strongly warn against the use of route ATK. I am addressing the proposal on behalf of the Progress Party and the Right.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e3154	The majority of the Committee on Transport has called for the reopening of the route measurement of speed. This will allow road authorities to take a very effective action against speed-related traffic accidents again. I would like to start by thanking the Christian People's Party and the Left for supporting the proposal. The reactions to the proposal were not expected from those working with road safety. Jan Johansen, director of Trygg Trafikk, said in his press release: ‘This is a happy decision. Stretching ATK saves lives and needs to be implemented.’ UP Chief Knut Danielsen pronounced to Rana Blade: ‘It is a real innovation for everyone working for road safety that the majority of the Committee on Transport and Communications today is in favour of refocusing on the average measurement of speed over longer distances with photoboxes.’ I think it is alarming that we have a government and a Minister of Transport who have stopped the possibility of using this effective tool. If we're going to reach the zero vision target, it's important that we apply a wide range of measures. Last year the measure was evaluated by the Transport Economic Institute and the conclusion was 49-54 pst. reduction of death and injury. The findings are consistent with previous evaluations — not just this one — and confirmed in international studies. High speed is one of the most important factors in the explanation of fatal accidents. Stretching ATK and normal ATK are effective supplements to the police's regular checks. The government must now reverse its decision to stop the further use of the average speed measuring photo boxes. The government must also reverse the refusal of the application by the Directorate of Road to set up average measurements in the Ellingsø tunnel and the Valderø tunnel in Møre and Romsdal. The zero vision and the national targets for further reduction in the number of road fatalities and serious injuries in the next few years are ambitious targets and require us to use the entire toolbox with measures to achieve the targets. The government said in the government statement that they want to reduce the use of the route ATK, but said at the same time that they would evaluate this. The evaluation is done, it was finished last autumn. The conclusion was, as I said, a reduction of up to 54 pst. of severely damaged and killed. Despite this, in March this year, the Ministry of Transport rejected, without justification, a request from the Directorate of Road to set up the route ATK in the Ellingsøjtunnel and the Valderøjtunnel on Rv. 658 in More and Romesdal. The tunnels are very accident-prone. 78 people have been killed or seriously injured since the opening in 1987 up to and including 2013. On-site measurements have shown that the average speed is very high and the police cannot carry out checks in these tunnels. The majority of the Committee therefore gave a decision requiring this Decision to be converted. The Minister has also stopped the application for section ATK on fv. 170 in Akershus. Akershu County Municipality has adopted the route ATK on a short distance of fv. 170. Eleven people have died and several dozen have been injured since 2000 on this 5.9 km long stretch between Heia and Mork. Now this application can also be carried out. Targeted work on increased road safety over time has produced good results, but too many people are still being killed and severely damaged in traffic. Traffic accidents lead to extreme suffering for the people affected and cost society huge sums. It is therefore incomprehensible that the government has sidelined and does not support this proposal, which is one of the means of achieving zero vision. The working party considers that both road standard and vehicle characteristics are important road safety measures. We will therefore continue to invest in both expansion and maintenance as well as in measures for further renewal of the car park. The Progress Party and the Right argue that they would rather build and maintain roads and give priority to road safety measures such as enhanced mid-pointing, road lighting and control of heavy-duty cars. The argumentation is problematic. All these measures are good – we agree with them – but they work differently. Those measures should therefore not be set out against each other. Route ATK is a local measure on accident-prone routes where high speed is a problem. It does not help to control heavy-duty vehicles on Svinesund whose problem is high speed in the tunnels in Møre and Romsdal. Our national targets are ambitious and therefore the entire toolbox needs to be used. Despite the happy reduction in traffic deaths to date in 2015 and good figures in 2014, it is important that we are not satisfied. We must pursue all the road safety gains we can, in the short, medium and long term.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e3200	The work on road safety consists in taking many measures at the same time. It is important to have a broad approach to this work. The government has announced that it will present a comprehensive Road Safety Communication to the Stortinget in 2016. That message will represent a milestone in road safety work, and the Stortinget will be invited to a complete discussion on the topic and not just to consider each action separately. It is good to see that we are now at a level of heavy damage and killing in traffic, which is the lowest since the 1950s, despite dramaticly many more vehicles on the roads. The development for 2015 is currently looking good. It shows that many of the measures taken actually work, but it's a reminder that we're not in the goal of the zero vision. Every single person we lose in traffic is one too much. Since October 2013, the government has implemented several measures that have improved road safety. The most important thing is the increased commitment to the maintenance and renewal of our roads. For the first time in several decades, we are now reducing the maintenance run. This means that the roads become more accessible, but also that they become safer to drive on. The investment in road construction has never been greater. When we also this week discussed Report. St. 25 for 2014–2015, On the right track, it represents a historical separation of time in the development of non-conference roads in the most populous areas of the country. In addition, the construction of more mid-driving works and the strengthening of the mid-driving marking of existing roads are more important than the objective of the national transport plan. Only in 2014 has a mid-point gain of 212 km of highway been established, while the target was 74 km. The latter is a consequence of the government's service in terms of road cover. Several non-meeting roads and enhanced mid-pointing may be the most effective measure we can take to reduce accident rates. This government has also quadrupled the number of checks on heavy-duty vehicles, and new technologies are being introduced to make these checks more effective. In addition, the consequences for non-compliances have become more stringent. This will undoubtedly have a preventative effect and will mean that less and less will take the chance of driving illegally, with the positive effect it will have on road safety. There is no doubt that there is a link between speed and number of accidents, as well as measures to check that the drivers stay within the speed limit, and awareness-raising work is important in this context. Increased bets on random checks and more visible police are probably what has the most preventative effect, as it is unpredictable and therefore something that we drivers have to take into account on the whole drive. So for distance-ATK. Automatic speed measurement has already been established in many places in the country, approx. 300 photo-boxes. On 23 sections, so-called distance ATK is established. The conditions for establishing average measurement are already relatively strict today. There is a sufficient need to limit this type of instrument to sections which are particularly exposed to accidents, while at the same time the use of route ATKs must be viewed against other road safety measures. The use of the route ATK is likely to have a significant impact on speed reduction, but at the same time it can contribute to unnatural driving patterns because many reduce speed unnecessarily and thus can contribute to creating both impatience and dangerous situations in traffic. We're not opposed to using the distance-ATK in some cases. As the Council of State also points out in its reply to the Committee, it can, for example: be appropriate to use this type of speed measurement in tunnels where it is difficult to control speed by other means. It is an assessment that must be made on a case-by-case basis, and I trust that it has been done for the two tunnels in Ålesund. There will be a totally hopeless practice if Stortinget starts to deal with the location of each photo box, as it is now put up in this case. For this reason, the Right and Advance Party have made a proposal asking the government to assess the criteria and guidelines for the use of ATK and route ATC within the framework of the work on a comprehensive Road Safety Communication. The Stortinget will then be able to review the legislation on a principle basis and in a perspective where the use of automatic speed measurement is assessed in the context of other instruments in road safety work. I am convinced that we will provide a better result for road users than we will assess the location of each photo box in this room.

ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e3392	From an accident perspective, 2014 was a historic year for Norwegian transport policy. We have to go all the way back to the beginning of the 1950s to figure out so low numbers for the number of casualties and hard damage. Of course, this is a happy and very good development, especially in the light of current congestion. Today there are about three million cars in Norway, but in the early 1950s there were hardly any cars on the roads. So far in 2015, accident figures continue to go downhill. It is interesting to note that such historically low figures of serious traffic accidents were achieved at the same time as the Right-Fremskritspart government stopped the development of the route ATK and used the resources of other road safety measures. What does that show? Well, it shows that roadside measures are most likely to have a better impact than ATK. Within Norwegian transport policy, we have implemented a number of measures to reduce the accidents. I can mention some – such as the repair of side terrain, the focus on driving behaviour, mid-drawing works, width expansion, double yellow barrier line and rumbling fields. Only such a simple but ingenious measure as a rumen field and more yellowing has shown up to 40 pst. reduction in meeting accidents. ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e3970	This case concerns the inclusion in the EEA Agreement of Regulation No. 452/2014 laying down technical requirements and administrative procedures for air operations performed by third country operators. In the Transport and Communications Committee, a Joint Committee has underlined that an authorisation under the Regulation does not in itself confer on third country operators the right to operate in the EU/EEA territory. Operators must apply for traffic permits in accordance with existing aviation agreements. This means that for Norway, the Air Safety Authority will continue to have the final word. The government has pointed out that EASA will be able to make a more thorough assessment of aviation safety than the individual EU/EEA States have the capacity to do separately. EASA will have extensive experience in the processing of applications, which will in the long term provide the Agency with a better basis for carrying out safety assessments than individual national supervisory authorities can. By setting common air safety requirements, to be operated by EASA on behalf of all EU/EEA States, the regulatory framework could in the long term increase aviation safety in the EU/EEA area. The Committee on Transport and Communications has also shown that one believes that there is a strong need for common requirements for air safety. The regulatory framework could lead to an administrative savings for the Air Safety Authority in addition to the fact that the assessment by EASA could provide a very good basis for the assessment by the Air Safety Authority of traffic rights. The Committee has pointed out that the methods to be used for applications and studies will be more extensive than those practised in Norway today. A Joint Committee also highlights the importance of Norway taking advantage of its right to meet, propose and express its opinion on the EASA Board, so that Norwegian interests can be developed in a good way in the future. In view of the comments I have now referred to from the Committee on Transport and Communications, I will again point out that one here sees it as such that this inclusion in the EEA Agreement of the Regulation laying down technical requirements and administrative procedures for aviation companies will strengthen aviation safety in Norway, and it will also create a more efficient system that is more holistic, including in the context of the whole of Europe.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e3992	This case is about Norway going to give birth to a supranational body of which we are not a member, the independent EU agency EASA. Should EASA be able to verify and provide authorisation for the safety of third-country operators engaged in commercial air transport in, to and out of the EU/EEA area? The reason why the Central Party and the Socialist Left have its own comments in the Foreign and Defence Committee is not primarily due to the health and great scepticism of EASA cooperation – although it may also be present – but to the relationship with the Constitution. In five cases under the EEA Agreement in 1992, the size has transferred decision-making competence to EU bodies using the procedure under the second paragraph of Section 26 of Grundlova. The size itself suggests that such a transfer has its place in the so-called ‘little intrusive’ transfer of sovereignty. The central party is not in agreement with this legal basis because, for the first time, there is no support or any basis in the Constitution for its text; for the second, it is contrary to the Constitutional Law of 1962 as well as to the Constitutional Law system, and for the third, it is contrary to and undermines the decision-making procedure of the Constitutional Law § 115. In short, the ‘small-invasive’ transfer of sovereignty has no support in any legal source other than the Stortinge’s own practice in contravention of Grundlova’s clear words and purposes. We are outside the Storting's legitimate room of interpretation when it comes to Constitution. If the Stortinget wants to transfer decision-making competence without using § 115, the Stortinget must change the Constitution. The connection to the connection condition is the following: How can the Storting with simple multiples according to a rule that is not legal, do something explicitly forbidden in Grundlova § 115, even with three-quarters majority? This is what I am asking the Council of State to do well for today. In the light of my view and the view of the Centre, Stortinget does not make its current practice meaningful in the light of the conditions of membership of § 115. Neither the Constitutional Disbursements of the Ministry of Justice, the Government nor the Grand Duchy of Justice have attempted to explain why Constitutional Laws should allow the transfer of sovereignty to EU bodies, with the exception of non-statutory ones, which are controversial, so-called ‘small-scale’ transfers of sovereignty. Another question is: Would the Stortinget have been able to make today's decision under § 115? In connection with Norway's application for membership of the European Community, in the 1960s, the Stortinget received contributions from Professors Torkel Opsahl and Johs. Andenæs. Both concluded that the ‘closed’ condition required Norway to be represented in the international body on equal terms with other States. There is also a point of view that is clearly dominant in later legal literature. In Arne Fliflet's commentary version to Grundlova, it is simply stated that § 115 only opens up the transfer of decision-making competence to organisations in Norway are in or are in the process of becoming a member. The general principle of international cooperation is that states participate on a similar line, albeit with different participation in voice numbers according to population and the like. It is this EEA Agreement that is based on – equal representation – as all decisions directly affecting Norway, Iceland and Liechtenstein are to be made by bodies where those States are fully and equally members – ESA, the EFTA Court and the EEA Committee. Looking at the preliminary work of § 115 from 1962, it seemed clear that the Stortinget then understood the word «closed» as a requirement for membership or participation on equal terms. Given this use of the word ‘closed’ in the pre-work, we must expect that the Storting had made a special comment about what they meant by the same word in Section 115. In addition to a broad agreement in legal literature, the elements imply that Norway must formally be a member of, or otherwise be represented on equal terms with, other States of the EASA Aviation Agency in order to be closed under Section 115. In other words, as long as Norway only has the right of meeting, proposal and questioning on the board of EASA — and much less so on the basis of Section 26 — the Stortinget may not transfer to EASA the decision-making competence of Norway. One might wonder how long the majority of population will continue to break the Constitution in their arduous drive to bring about EU integration. Where is the logic that requires three-fourths of a plural number to give away sovereignty to an organization we are a member of, while, as the plural suggests, that simple plural numbers are sufficient to give away sovereignty to an organization we are not a member of? I would like to deal with the proposal from the Centre Party.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e4013	The representative, Liv Signe Navarsete, has taken up the proposal she referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2015-06-18.ana.ud1556e4016	First of all, on the content of the regulation: this is a regulation requiring aircraft operators, i.e.: air carriers from non-EU/EEA countries need the approval of the European Aviation Safety Agency, EASA, in order to operate within the EU/EEA area. EASA shall ensure that third-country operators comply with the common European requirements for such operations. The authorisations of EASA will apply to the entire EU/EEA area. The authorisations replace the current regime, whereby third-country operators must obtain authorisation from each EU/EEA country in which they wish to operate. They're getting a clean security permit, it's not a traffic permit. They have to get it from every state to which they want to fly. There is no automatic means of obtaining a traffic permit, even if they have received a safety authorisation. In the case of the Constitution: Norway joined EASA already in 2005 by incorporating the EASA Regulation into the EEA Agreement. Norway thus agreed in 2005 that EASA will have a direct effect on Norwegian companies involved in the design and construction of equipment for the aircraft industry. While only a handful of such permits have been granted, it shows that the Storting has already agreed in 2005 that a supranational decision-making authority is sensible in relation to specific and strictly factually defined areas, such as aviation safety, and the Constitution allows it. So this is a practice that has been in force under the government of which Navarsete was part. That perception was actually repeated in Prop. 27 S for 2012-2013 under the red-green government when the Stortinget agreed to the inclusion of a new version of EASA Regulation into the EEA Agreement. It's a little strange that what is now suddenly problematic was quite fine when the party of Navarsette itself crashed. The starting point now is that one from an aviation safety professional perspective is associated with EASA in an area that gives a common approach across the EU/EEA area. That means, in sum, that one could have increased aviation safety in our part of the world. I think that is positive. There are no rights to fly to Norway, but there is a safety assessment that makes it safer to fly in Europe. I think that is positive. So this is an organ that is not very interventional, and then, with the consent of Storting, it can be adopted with the consent of the second paragraph of Section 26 of the Constitution by requiring a simple majority. Air traffic surveillance will continue to be able to refuse the authorisation of operators to fly to and from Norway on grounds relating to the market situation. Air traffic control may also assess the assurances of the operator and one may refuse to operate in Norway when unforeseen safety concerns arise. This means that the Air Safety Authority has the possibility to sanction, even if it has received a safety authorisation, when a local authority considers that the situation so warrants. I am pleased that the Stortinge makes this decision that gives us safer aviation in Europe. There's nothing new in the way you decide, compared to what was adopted just a few years ago when the Senter Party had the Minister of Transport.

In the meantime, it has been suggested that projects and actions in the Pre-package should provide better conditions for both transit and local traffic. This will be achieved, among others. by separating pedestrian and bicycle traffic from traffic. It is a goal to establish a coherent bicycle road network. There will also be built download routes. Several of the projects will facilitate development and development in and near the centre. There is local political support for a partial toll financing scheme for the Pre-package. A joint committee agrees that the established toll company should be allowed to charge tolls for the partial toll financing of the Sogn and Fjordane Pre-package. It is estimated that the construction of the first project will start approximately at the same time as the toll collection begins, i.e. a so-called parallel toll collection. With the financial contributions for the Pre-package, a tolling period of 12 years has been introduced. In accordance with the Toll Agreement, the right to collect tolls will lapse once the company has fulfilled its obligations. Upon collection, any excess funds will be put at the disposal of the State Road Service and may be made available to the national road system in the area where the tolls are required. The decision in principle was taken in Förde Municipality in 2011, and one of the projects has been ready for implementation for less than four years. It's a milestone in itself. I would like to thank the locals who have worked locally for E39 and Förde to get the fastest possible solution to the traffic challenges they face. I am pleased that today we can make a decision for the Pre-package, so that Sogn and Fjordane can start a long-term project and action.
